The RED carried a real token in the operator-facing detail (`.superpowers/run-p-hard1-t1-red2.log`: `... ?X-Plex-Token=SECRETTOKEN returned 500` inside `str(CollectionsPassFailed)`, i.e. verbatim what `scheduled_runs.last_detail` stored). Tested at the service surface and the job surface (`tests/test_scheduler_collections_job.py`). No consumer of the old `str(error)` was left broken — `LibraryOutcome.error` is read only by `service.py`'s own `ok` and `summary`. The operator-visible change is real and intended: a failed library now reads `Movies: failed (RuntimeError)` on the served surfaces, with the message and traceback one hop away in the pod log. Closed together with row 136, which is the same line. | S — redact at the rollback boundary, plus a test per surface | none (a leak, not a feature) | — |
| 189 | `original_language` and `origin_country` are dynamic types nothing here can enumerate (CLOSED — the prefetch phase, Phase A) | Filed by phase 10a-1's wrap, out of adjudication C3's OUT list, so the two types the engine visibly lacks are a row somebody can read rather than a silence. Both ARE Kometa dynamic types (`meta.py:36-37`) and neither is a library enumeration: upstream builds them as PLAIN collections whose membership comes from a full-library **TMDb walk** — every item's TMDb record fetched, `original_language` / `origin_country` read off it, and the distinct values collected (`meta.py:971-993`, `builder.py:360-374`). So they are not one `listFilterChoices` call short of shipping; they need the metadata-prefetch budget the same rows 155/180 keep running into — a per-item external fetch across the whole library, with its own cache, its own rate limit and its own failure mode when TMDb does not know an item. Plex's own `<Country>` is NOT a substitute: it is the production country as Plex's agent recorded it, which is what the shipped movie-only `country` type already enumerates (row 174), while `origin_country` is TMDb's field and the two disagree on exactly the co-productions an operator would notice. Deliberately not built in 10a-1, and 10a-2 does not want it either — this is 10b/10c company, once someone decides what a whole-library TMDb walk is allowed to cost. **CLOSED by the prefetch phase (Phase A), and the answer to "what is a whole-library TMDb walk allowed to cost" turned out to be nothing.** Every clause of the blocker above is now shipped, taken in this row's own order. **The per-item external fetch: unchanged, and that is the finding.** There is no walk. The three fields ride the `/movie/{id}` and `/tv/{id}` reads the facts pipeline was ALREADY making and discarding — `_countries` and `_language` (`facts/tmdb_facts.py:60`, `:78`) read them off the details payload the parser already had, so the budget bought **zero new requests**. That `origin_country` is present on `/movie/{id}` at all was settled by live capture before anything was planned around it, not assumed: the recon's caution (it was a later TMDb addition, and might have needed `append_to_response` or a second endpoint) did not hold. `_countries` declines a bare string, because `"US"` would otherwise iterate to `["U", "S"]` — a plausible wrong value, which is worse than an absent one. **Its own cache:** `provider_cache`, already there, and a refusal never enters it — `providers/fetch.py` writes the cache *below* `raise_for_status` (`:47` then `:50`), so a 429 body cannot become an answer with a TTL. That is row 147's MDBList defect refused by construction rather than by care, and a mutation moving the write above the raise turns the assertion red. **Its own rate limit:** `tmdb_rate_state` (a single row pinned to `id=1`) and `operations.tmdb_backoff_seconds` (default 60; `0` disables the shared window and is today's behaviour byte for byte). Both sides of every comparison come from the DATABASE clock, so pods behind one database share one window instead of each discovering the 429 for itself — `ImdbMissRefreshState`'s pattern, cited at the site. `Retry-After` is honoured in its delta-seconds form only (parsing the HTTP-date form would mean trusting a *remote* clock to set a window kept on the *database* clock), capped at an hour against a hostile header, and a refusal WIDENS an open window rather than overwriting it. **Its own failure mode when TMDb does not know an item:** `MediaItem.facts_attempted_at` is now stamped on every attempt, unconditionally and above the empty-gather short-circuit (`facts/gather.py:184`) — so "TMDb has nothing for this item" and "nobody has looked" stopped being the same two NULLs, which is exactly the state the enumeration's missing-value rule reads. **What shipped ON that budget:** `builder: facts_family`, with `origin_country` and `original_language` as two of its three types, both library types each. Not two `DYNAMIC_TYPES` rows, and the reason is structural rather than stylistic — it is this row's own first sentence read one layer down: a facts-enumerated type's MEMBERSHIP cannot be a Plex smart filter, because Plex has no `origin_country` field, so a row in that table would be a row its builder cannot build and, worse, one the params validator would advertise to an operator as an option (`src/autoposter/collections/facts_family.py`'s module docstring states this whole argument). What IS shared is everything that matters: `dynamic_keys.derive_keys` and `dynamic_titles.family_titles` are byte-unchanged and have never known where the values came from, so Kometa's addon merges, its `include` whitelist, its `other` leftovers bucket and its duplicate-title refusal are phase 10a's, reused rather than rebuilt. **Coverage is honest rather than complete, and stays that way by design:** the family enumerates what the facts pipeline has VISITED, so a half-swept library builds a half-sized family — correct, incomplete, converging. At the shipped defaults (`scheduler.drift_batch_size` 500 every `scheduler.drift_days` 7) a 16,000-item library is worked through in 32 weeks. **That is arithmetic over two shipped defaults, not a measurement, and no coverage percentage appears anywhere in this roadmap** — the only database this phase could reach was the empty isolated test one, which has no schema at all. What an operator gets instead is per-pass and true: a family that builds nothing reports both numbers ("visited N of M item(s) there") in the pass's own actions | M–L — a budgeted per-item TMDb walk with its own cache, then two type-table rows | parity-only | 102, 155, 180 |
| 190 | A dynamic language family is named by Plex, not by TMDb's ISO table (CLOSED — the location-names phase) | Filed by phase 10a-1's wrap as its own row because it is the phase's one behavioural divergence from upstream that an operator can SEE, and a divergence that lives only in a module docstring is one nobody finds. Upstream titles an `audio_language` / `subtitle_language` bucket from TMDb's `_iso_639_1` table — `en` becomes "English" — and falls back to the Plex choice's own title when TMDb has no entry (`meta.py:928-929`). This service had no such table at filing, so it shipped upstream's fallback branch as its only branch (`src/autoposter/collections/dynamic_types.py`). **Membership is unaffected and provably so:** the KEY is `choice.key` either way, so only the collection's NAME differs, and `title_override` sets it by hand today. On the production libraries the two agree for the common codes (`en=English`, `fi=Finnish`); where they part is the locale variants Plex already titles well — `es-419` is "Spanish (Latin America)" — so it is not obvious the TMDb table is even the better name. Closing this meant vendoring the ISO table (or reading TMDb's `/configuration/languages`), a data dependency for a cosmetic gain, which is why it was filed rather than built. **Which branch phase 10b's two language packs took, recorded here so a reader of this row does not have to go and look:** the Plex-names branch, with the ISO table still unvendored at that time. Neither `media_audio_language` nor `media_subtitle_language` shipped a `title_override` table, and not as a shortcut — there was nothing to transcribe. Upstream's `audio_language.yml`/`subtitle_language.yml` carry no code→name mapping at all (the names come from `meta.py:928`'s run-time `_iso_639_1` lookup), so a transcribed table would have been invented rather than copied, which is exactly what the catalog's provenance rule refuses. What the packs DO carry is upstream's own one-entry `key_name_override` — `myn` → "Mayan", the single non-ISO-639-1 code in the 187 whose TMDb lookup misses — transcribed as the `key_name` the `title_format` then wraps ("Mayan Audio"), not as a finished title. **CLOSED by the location-names phase — half by shipping, half by decision, and the "cosmetic gain not worth a data dependency" reasoning above is superseded rather than merely dated.** The dependency stopped being this row's to justify: row 196 needed the same fetch for a reason that was not cosmetic at all (two packs that could not ship), so the table arrived as that row's cost and this one names it for free. It exists now — TMDb's `/configuration/languages` `english_name` table, fetched once and vendored at `collections/iso_names.py` as `LANGUAGE_NAMES`. The family that had NO title source at all uses it: `original_language` buckets are titled through the table with the code-as-title fallback for any code it misses, keys untouched (row 156's law). One wrinkle was found by measuring the table rather than trusting it: TMDb gives both `nr` and `nd` the name "Ndebele", and because this family keys on the CODE, two keys would claim one title and `DuplicateFamilyTitle` would refuse EVERY language collection, not just the clashing pair. `language_title` appends the code to any name more than one code carries — "Ndebele (nr)", "Ndebele (nd)" — a suffix that is OURS but invents no vocabulary, being the family key every narrowing knob already speaks. **The two shipped Plex-enumerated families deliberately KEEP Plex's own titles**, and that is a decision this row's own evidence argues for, not a shortfall: this row observed that Plex titles the locale variants better — `es-419` is "Spanish (Latin America)", which a bare ISO-639-1 table cannot say — so renaming two shipped families onto a worse-informed source is refused. The table is one import away if that judgement ever changes, and both pack descriptions still say who names their buckets | S — vendor or fetch the ISO-639-1 name table, then one lookup with the existing fallback | parity-only (names only, never membership) | 102, 196 (the fetch that supplied the table) |
| 191 | Eight forked Plex `section` doubles in the collections suite | Filed by phase 10a-2's wrap out of that phase's Task 4 review (C4 / Minor 7). 24 test modules across the suite define their own `Section` double (`grep -rn "^class .*Section" tests/*.py`); eight are what the Common Sense port had to edit to grow the same two members — a filter choice carrying `key` as well as `title`, and the raw POST/PUT routes: `tests/test_builder_port_golden.py`, `test_builder_settings.py`, `test_collection_main.py`, `test_collection_poster_wiring.py`, `test_collection_reconcile.py`, `test_collection_separator.py`, `test_scheduler_collections_job.py`. (`test_collection_adoption.py` needed the same two-member edit too but is not one of the 24 — it imports its `Section` fakes, `FakeSection as ListSection`/`FakeSection as SmartSection`, from `test_collection_lists.py`/`test_collection_reconcile.py` rather than defining its own, `:22,:24`.) The next change to how this family talks to Plex costs the same eight edits. Deliberately NOT consolidated inside the port: a shared-fixture refactor would have landed in the very commit the golden gate grades, which is the adjacent refactor the review forbids. One wrinkle to carry into the consolidation: two of the eight (`test_collection_main.py`, `test_scheduler_collections_job.py`) dropped their `createCollection` fake; `test_collection_reconcile.py` deliberately kept its own ON PURPOSE, to prove plexapi is never called, and `test_builder_settings.py` also still carries one (`:230-234`) — so an accidental regression in the two that dropped it surfaces as an `AttributeError` several frames from the cause rather than as a readable assertion, and the shared double has to keep the readable proof somewhere | S–M — one shared section double and eight call sites; no production code | none (tests only) | 185 |
| 192 | `tmdb_collection` is a dynamic type nothing here can enumerate (CLOSED — the prefetch phase, Phase A) | Filed by phase 10b, out of adjudication C1, because `content_franchises` had been gated on row 102 and row 102 closed without being able to help it. Kometa's franchise pack is `dynamic_collections: {tmdb_collection: ...}` — one collection per TMDb franchise collection the library's items belong to, with its addon merges (Prometheus into Alien, Minions into Despicable Me) and its "Collection" suffix stripped. It is not a library enumeration: `listFilterChoices` has no field for it, and `collections/dynamic_types.py`'s scope note already names it among the deliberate absences (`dynamic_types.py:38-39`). Getting the value set means reading every item's TMDb record and collecting the `belongs_to_collection` ids — the same metadata-prefetch budget rows 155/180/189 keep running into, with its own cache, rate limit and not-found behaviour. The single-collection `tmdb_collection` BUILDER is already shipped (phase 8b), so what is missing is exactly the enumeration and nothing else. Deliberately not built in 10b: adding an enumeration seam was out of that phase's scope by adjudication C1. **CLOSED by the prefetch phase (Phase A).** The budget this row shares with 155/180/189 shipped there and is described clause by clause in row 189 — the same `provider_cache`, the same `tmdb_rate_state` / `operations.tmdb_backoff_seconds` window on the database clock, the same `facts_attempted_at` stamp that makes "TMDb has nothing for this item" a state distinct from "nobody has looked". Two things are this row's own rather than 189's. **First, the enumeration cost nothing extra here either:** `belongs_to_collection.id` is read off the same `/movie/{id}` payload by `_collection_id` (`facts/tmdb_facts.py:91`), and `null` — the value a film in no franchise genuinely carries, captured directly rather than inferred — is read as *absent*, never as a shape error. `/tv/{id}` has no `belongs_to_collection` key at all, which is why the type is movie-only and not a preference. **Second, what this row said was missing is precisely what was added and nothing else:** the 8b single-collection `tmdb_collection` builder is untouched and is what each unit is built BY, so the family's members are the franchise's own `parts` rather than the items this service happens to have fetched. The one thing the enumeration alone cannot supply is the NAME — the captured `belongs_to_collection` object carries exactly four keys and no `parts`, so `providers/tmdb_lists.collection_name` reads `/collection/{id}`, sharing one `provider_cache` row with the `collection_parts` call the builder already makes (proven by a test counting requests, not asserted). A franchise TMDb cannot name is dropped and REPORTED with the `title_override` that would name it by hand; only the 404 degrades, because "TMDb was unreachable this pass" is not "this franchise has no name". It ships as `builder: facts_family`'s third type rather than as a `DYNAMIC_TYPES` row, for the structural reason row 189 sets out. `content_franchises` flips to READY on it (see row 199 for the two ways the family diverges from Kometa's) | M–L — a budgeted per-item TMDb walk, then one dynamic-type row | parity-only | 189 |
| 193 | `tests/test_worker.py` fails intermittently on full-suite runs — four different tests, six sightings | Filed by phase 10b's wrap because the FILE has now been seen **four times across two phases** as of 10b's wrap, and a fifth time since, on the deferred-jobs branch (see the dated note at the end of this row) — two 10a-2-era sightings (its Task 1 dispatch run, and its whole-branch review, raised there as a ⚠️ and not as a failure) and two of 10b's own, an hour apart on 2026-08-27: Task 4's full-suite run (`.superpowers/run-t4-full1.log`, 22:22:17 — `test_db_error_in_handler_reschedules_instead_of_stranding_at_running` fails with `AttributeError: 'NoneType' object has no attribute 'lower'` on `job.last_error`) and Task 5's full-suite run (`.superpowers/run-t5-full.log`, 23:23:08 — `test_a_cancelled_job_that_succeeds_still_completes` fails with `assert job.state == "done"` getting `pending`) — and a fourth sighting inside one phase is where "someone's flaky machine" stops being the honest reading. **Two different tests, not one repeating shape.** The row was originally filed on the T5 assertion alone; T4's is a different test failing a different assertion, not a repeat of it — but it is the SAME class of defect, a `run_once` write the test's session cannot see (there, the state transition; here, the error bookkeeping written alongside a reschedule), and it is, if anything STRONGER evidence for this row's own transaction-visibility hypothesis: the invisible-write shape recurs across two independent paths through `run_once`, not one assertion misbehaving twice. **What the isolation evidence rules out**, attributed to the sighting each re-run actually followed: it is not a regression from any of this work (`tests/test_worker.py` is untouched by the 10a-1, 10a-2 and 10b branches, appearing in none of their test-file diffs, and the worker is orthogonal to the collections subsystem all three phases changed); it is not deterministic (the file alone re-ran green immediately after the **T4** sighting — `.superpowers/run-t4-worker.log`, 20 passed, 22:22:46 — and the FULL suite re-ran green end to end after the **T5** sighting — `.superpowers/run-t5-full2.log`, 3702 passed 0 failed, 23:34:48 — each with no code change between its own failure and its own green re-run; no file-alone re-run after the T5 sighting is evidenced on disk); and it is not the shared-database collision this project has hit before, since `tests/conftest.py`'s `_database_for_this_process` gives every xdist worker its own database (`<base>_gw0`, `_gw1`, …), so no second worker can be claiming this job. What that leaves — unproven, and named as a hypothesis rather than a diagnosis — is a timing or transaction-visibility race between a test's setup writes and `run_once`'s claim, of the kind a loaded container surfaces and a quiet one does not. **Provenance, stated honestly.** The two 10b sightings each have a durable full-suite log on disk (`run-t4-full1.log`, `run-t5-full.log`, both cited above). The two 10a-2-era sightings have no durable artifact in the repo; they rest on the session ledger's contemporaneous record, `.superpowers/sdd/progress.md`, as their sole provenance. **Why it is a row and not a fix:** every sighting so far has been noticed by a phase whose gate was "full suite green", re-run once, and passed; nobody has yet reproduced it on demand, and a speculative `await`-and-retry in the worker would make the tests pass without anyone knowing whether the production claim path shares the race. The work is to reproduce first — loop **the file**, not the one test, under the suite's own concurrency until it fails, capture the job row's timestamps for whichever test trips — and only then decide whether the defect is in a test's setup or in `run_once`'s claim path. **Fifth sighting, 2026-08-29** (deferred-jobs fix round): a third distinct test, `test_a_cancelled_job_waiting_for_plex_is_also_dismissed`, failed on a full-suite run — one-off, with the file re-run alone coming back 22/22 and a clean full-suite rerun following it, both with no code change in between. Recorded on the session ledger (`.superpowers/sdd/progress.md`), same as the two 10a-2-era sightings, with no durable full-suite log of its own beyond that entry. **Sixth sighting, 2026-08-29** (the prefetch phase, Phase A — Task 2's full-suite run): a FOURTH distinct test, `test_cancelled_mid_db_operation_still_releases_and_propagates`, failed with `Failed: DID NOT RAISE CancelledError` (`test_worker.py:150`) in a run of 1 failed / 3761 passed. Re-run alone with no code change: 22 passed. It has a **durable full-suite log** — `.superpowers/run-ppf-t2-full.log` — which the fifth and the two 10a-2-era sightings do not, and it is unrelated to the work that saw it (the prefetch phase touched the facts pipeline, the TMDb client and the collections engine; the worker is orthogonal to all three, and `tests/test_worker.py` is untouched by the branch). One sighting across this branch's full-suite runs; every other one came back clean. **A methodological finding that belongs in this row, because it bears on the sighting COUNT rather than on any one sighting.** The container command that produced that log is `pytest -q | tee …`, so `docker wait` returned **0 despite the failure** — the exit code was tee's. Any earlier phase that gated a full-suite run on the container's exit status rather than on reading the log file could have passed over a sighting without seeing it, which means the six recorded here are a floor and not a census. Reading the log rather than trusting the wait is now the phase discipline, and it is how this sighting was caught at all | S — reproduce under a loop, then one fix in either a test's setup or the claim path | parity-only (test infrastructure; no shipped behaviour known to be affected) | — |
| 194 | The appearance-threshold dynamic person types, and the library-wide credit scan they need (CLOSED — phase B) | Filed by phase 10c-lite, out of adjudication C1, because row 83 SPLIT three ways: its static half (the five filmography builders) shipped in 8c, its bio/poster half shipped in 10c-lite, and this — the expensive half — never got the argument row 192 got for `tmdb_collection`. **What upstream's packs actually are.** All four (`defaults/both/actor.yml`, `defaults/movie/director.yml`, `defaults/movie/writer.yml`, `defaults/movie/producer.yml`) are `dynamic_collections` with `data: {depth: 5, limit: 25}` — one collection per person with at least five appearances in the library, capped at the twenty-five with the most — and **the packs name no people at all** (all four transcribed whole in `.superpowers/sdd/p10c-upstream-person.md` §2.1; the same reading is written above `_STARTER_DIRECTORS` in `src/autoposter/collections/catalog.py`). That is why there is nothing to transcribe into a preset, and why `people_directors` ships six directors that are openly OURS and marked `NOT_KOMETA` in their own row — the only thing borrowed from upstream there is the `<name> (Director)` title shape. **Blocker one — row 169, and it is STRUCTURAL rather than merely missing.** `actor`, `director`, `writer` and `producer` are not rows of `FILTER_ATTRIBUTES`, so `filters.BY_NAME` (`src/autoposter/collections/filters.py:890`, built from that table) has no entry for any of them and the per-person query each of these collections would carry cannot be *written* at all today — not untested, inexpressible. And row 169 needs more than four table rows: Kometa's person resolution runs through `get_actor_id` (`builder.py:4426`), verified by the T1 review (`p10c-task-1-review.md:143-149`) to BE `hubSearch` filtered to `Role` (`plex.py:1273-1280`, the same citation row 169 already carries) — the same Plex person-tag machinery, not a conditional fallback — and it runs only AFTER `get_search_choices` at `builder.py:4412` has already been called and can already have raised. `builder.py:4400-4412` enters the tag branch and `builder.py:4412` → `plex.py:1300-1319` enumerates every actor tag in the library, raising when the library cannot produce the listing. **Blocker two — the credit scan, which is the rows 155/180 budget family.** NAMING the people means reading every credit of every item in the library: a Plex-side per-item people read, through a cache that does not exist yet. Whether that cache is `item_facts` or a table of its own is a DECISION this row records as a decision — made at design time, not discovered at review. **The counts problem, which no choices listing can answer.** `depth` is a count of appearances, and `listFilterChoices` returns VALUES, never counts — so even a library whose cast were enumerable would still not answer the question the pack asks. This is what distinguishes this row from rows 189 and 192, where the enumeration alone was the blocker. **The fan-out, against the number the 10a probe actually measured.** The worst measured enumeration is `studio` at **824** values (`docs/research/plex-dynamic-probe/README.md`), and a library's distinct cast is larger than its distinct studios by an order of magnitude — so this family meets `params.max_collections`' refusal (default 50, which creates nothing and reports both numbers) rather than a truncation. A cap that truncated would be worse than the refusal: it would build a plausible, arbitrary fraction of the family and look finished. **An open question whoever closes this row has to answer explicitly, because nobody has:** upstream's per-person collection is a search on the Plex ACTOR TAG — the library's own credit data — while a `tmdb_actor` filmography is TMDb's credit data intersected with the library. They are different memberships under the same name, and which one this service should build is a decision, not a detail. **`tmdb_popular_people` files here too, on the same evidence.** 10c-lite's decision gate returned VERDICT B-GATED positively rather than as a tie-break: upstream's `tmdb_popular_people` is a dynamic collection TYPE with no builder and no shipped pack (`popular_people` has zero matches in `modules/builder.py`; no `defaults/` file uses the type), and its default template is `meta.py:42` — `{"tmdb_person": "<<value>>", "plex_search": {"all": {"actor": "tmdb"}}}` — so each collection is one person's, given a biography and a photo by `tmdb_person` and its MEMBERS by a Plex actor-tag search. That search is the row-169 machinery again: `actor` is a Plex tag attribute (`plex.py:527`), the literal string `tmdb` is a placeholder only `tmdb_person`'s resolved NAMES can fill (`builder.py:4401-4408`, fed by `builder.py:1409`), and those names are then matched against the library's own actor-tag listing (`plex.py:1300-1319`). Four tag-free candidates were hunted and closed rather than assumed away: TMDb's `known_for` is discarded before Kometa can use it (`tmdb.py:505` keeps only `p.id` and `p.name`), the four shipped packs reach the identical tag branch by a second route through `templates.yml:238-255`'s `smart_filter` template, `meta.py`'s type lists offer no alternative shape, and `defaults/` ships no pack for the type at all. What could ship without the tag and deliberately does not: an expansion into per-person `tmdb_actor` filmographies is buildable TODAY and would be a DIFFERENT membership from upstream's — upstream's collection holds the library items TAGGED with that person, ours would hold the library items TMDb CREDITS them on — so shipping it under upstream's name would be the fabricated attribution this project's provenance rule forbids. If it ever ships, it ships as ours, `NOT_KOMETA`, with that difference stated in the row. Filing it costs no preset parity, because Kometa ships no default pack that uses it. **What 10c-lite DID ship, so this row is not misread as "people do not work":** the five filmography builders (8c) now carry the person's TMDb biography as their collection summary and their TMDb profile photo as its poster (`TmdbListClient.person_detail`, `posters.tmdb_profile_url`), every existing override still wins over both, and `people_directors` is a working preset today. Row 83 CLOSED with it. **Re-pointed by the prefetch phase's wrap.** The prefetch phase split the "metadata-prefetch budget" this row's dependency cell shared with 155/180 into its two real halves and shipped only the TMDb-side one (rows 189/192). That half buys this row nothing: the credit scan this row needs is Plex-side, it needs COUNTS across the whole library rather than a scalar per item, and the recon that scoped the phase named this row as the one consumer of the four that does not share the payload the other three ride on. The budgeted per-item credit scan and the "is the cache `item_facts` or a table of its own" decision are row 197's, taken there as a decision rather than discovered later **CLOSED — phase B, and the whole chain shipped rather than a part of it.** The scan is `collections/credits.scan_library_credits`, which walks the library's owned keys through the same batched `/library/metadata/{k1,k2,…}` read row 197 built (`plex/client.fetch_credit_index`, chunks of 200) and writes `item_credits` rows; it runs on its own `credits_scan` scheduler job at `scheduler.credits_scan_days: 7`, so the cost is a weekly library walk rather than anything a collections pass pays. The counted enumeration is `credits.enumerate_credits`, `facts_enumeration`'s own `GROUP BY` shape returning `(value, count)` — which is the thing `listFilterChoices` structurally cannot answer and the reason this row could not be a dynamic type. The family is a FOURTH builder shape, `builder: credits_family` (`collections/builders/credits_family.py`), one smart collection per person over row 169's four search attributes, taking upstream's own `data:` values as params (`depth: 5`, `limit: 25`). The four Top-* packs flip **READY**: `people_top_actors`, `people_top_directors`, `people_top_writers`, `people_top_producers` (`CATALOG_CHECKSUM["people"]` moves (1,4,0) → (5,0,0)). **The open question this row carried is answered, and the answer is disclosed rather than assumed:** the person key is the **Plex tag name**, because the feed is Plex's own library credit data — the phase-B probe's D1 gate measured Role/Director/Writer/Producer riding the batch (8815 roles across 200 movies) and identical to a single-key fetch on 6 of 6 sampled items, so no TMDb fallback was needed and no filmography-vs-tag divergence arises. **What the packs must NOT claim, and their descriptions say so:** the counts are a FLOOR, not a census. Plex caps `<Role>` at 200 children per item server-side, unescapable by single-key reads, so a person whose every appearance is in a >200-role cast can be absent from the ranking entirely — the ranking is biased against people who only ever appear in large casts. Shows carry **no** director/writer/producer at all (0 in the batch, 0 in a single-key fetch, 0 enumerable) — a property of series-level Plex metadata, those credits living on episodes, which is why the three crew kinds are Movie-only by name. `tmdb_popular_people` stays filed: nothing here changed its verdict, since a popularity chart is not a library enumeration | L — a budgeted per-item credit scan with its own cache, then row 169's person lookup, then one dynamic-type row | parity-only | 169, 197, 102 (engine, closed) |
| 195 | `tests/test_pipeline.py::test_second_upsert_of_the_same_rating_key_refreshes_updated_at` fails intermittently on a full-suite run — one sighting, with a mechanism note (CLOSED — hardening sweep 1) | Filed by phase 10c-lite's wrap as its own row rather than as a sixth sighting widening row 193, because nothing about it is row 193's: a different file, a different subsystem (the render pipeline's upsert, not the worker's claim path) and a different mechanism. **The sighting.** 2026-08-29, phase 10c-lite Task 2's first full-suite run (`.superpowers/run-p10c-t2-full.log`, `1 failed, 3731 passed in 566.63s`). The test writes one `rating_key` twice, `asyncio.sleep(1.1)` apart, and asserts `second.updated_at > first.updated_at`; it got `12:25:31.191867` for the SECOND write against `12:25:32.114473` for the first — the timestamps went BACKWARDS by ~0.92s across two commits separated by a 1.1-second sleep. Not deterministic and not a regression from that phase's work (it touches nothing in `render/`): the file alone re-ran 42/42 immediately after (`.superpowers/run-p10c-t2-pipeline-recheck.log`) and the full suite re-ran `3732 passed` end to end with no code change in between (`.superpowers/run-p10c-t2-full2.log`). Both logs are on disk, which is more provenance than three of row 193's five sightings have. **The mechanism note, which is this row's whole value.** PostgreSQL's `now()` is `transaction_timestamp()` — the moment the enclosing TRANSACTION began, not the moment the statement ran — and both writes take their value from it: the insert path from `MediaItem.updated_at`'s `server_default=func.now()` (`src/autoposter/db/models.py:66-68`) and the conflict path from `_upsert_media_item`'s explicit `set_={..., "updated_at": func.now()}` (`src/autoposter/render/pipeline.py:311`), which exists because SQLAlchemy's `onupdate=` hook never fires on an `INSERT … ON CONFLICT DO UPDATE`. Under those semantics the sleep guarantees nothing the assertion needs: what is being compared is the distance between two transaction STARTS, and a `now()` read on a pooled connection whose transaction began earlier returns a timestamp older than one issued before it. That is the only shape that produces the observed INVERSION rather than merely a narrowed gap — and it is a hypothesis with the numbers behind it, not a diagnosis; nobody has reproduced it on demand. **Why it is a row and not a fix**, the same argument row 193 makes: the tempting change (relax to `>=`, or lengthen the sleep) makes the test pass without anyone learning whether the production upsert's `updated_at` is trustworthy, and that column is read outside this test. The work is to reproduce first — loop the FILE under the suite's own concurrency, logging `txid_current()`, `transaction_timestamp()` and `clock_timestamp()` for both writes — and only then decide whether the defect is the assertion's premise or the column's semantics. Cross-reference row 193 as the same observational CLASS (full-suite-only, one-off, green on both the file-alone and the clean full re-run) and nothing more; row 193 itself gains nothing from this phase, whose runs produced no `tests/test_worker.py` sighting at all. **closed hardening-sweep-1: REPRODUCED, and the defect was the assertion's premise, not the column.** A 200-iteration loop of the file under `-n auto --dist loadgroup` (the sighting's own concurrency) failed on **iteration 1** (`.superpowers/run-p-hard1-loop195.log`). The three-clock diagnostic shows BOTH clocks inverted between the two writes — `transaction_timestamp()` **-1.581795 s**, `clock_timestamp()` **-1.570837 s** across a 1.1 s sleep — with `txid_current()` advancing normally (901 → 915, so no transaction reordering) and write1's own `clock` reading 1.8 ms EARLIER than its own `xact`, which is impossible under a monotonic clock and is an independent proof on its own. An independent 120 s probe of `clock_timestamp()` and the container's `time.time()` then measured the cause directly: the VM wall clock that sources both steps backwards by a composite **~2.705 s roughly every ~30 s of real time**, both clocks in lockstep to under 100 µs (the probe's raw 27.28 s cadence is APPARENT time, short by exactly one step per cycle). **The rate, stated correctly:** the bad window is the ~1.12 s interval between the test's two timestamp readings, not the step's magnitude — so exposure is `interval / 30 s` ≈ **3.7 % per execution**, and iteration 1 hitting it was an early draw, not what the rate predicts. Two independent corroborations close on the same step: -1.5818 + 2.705 = 1.1232 s real elapsed (the 1.1 s sleep plus ~23 ms of overhead), and the 2026-08-29 sighting's -0.92 s implies 1.785 s — the same step across an interval stretched wider by full-suite load, which is why the flake looked full-suite-only. Suite length cannot change a test's per-execution probability; it can and does widen the test's own inter-write interval. `clock_timestamp()` steps with it, so switching the column to it would not have helped. **Fixed in `9503df0`:** `assert second.updated_at != first.updated_at`. Mutation-proven still to catch the regression this test exists for — deleting the `set_` re-stamp yields byte-identical timestamps — and 30/30 loop iterations green afterwards (under the old `>` the chance of at least one failure over 30 iterations was ~68 %, not the ~96 % first reported). **The residual, named rather than denied:** `!=` cannot catch a re-stamp that writes an EARLIER value, which is precisely the alternative hypothesis (a `func.now()` serving a transaction start older than the row's existing value). On a machine with a sane clock `>` would catch that class; here it cannot distinguish that defect from the environment, so the relaxation is the minimum the reproduced mechanism licenses and the loss is recorded instead of claimed away. **Row 193: no opportunity to sight.** The loop ran `tests/test_pipeline.py` alone, so `tests/test_worker.py` could not have appeared — this is a null result by construction, not a health signal for row 193, and row 193's code was not touched. **What outlives this row:** `docs/research/dev-clock-step/README.md` (measurement, corrected derivation, reproducer, and the enforceable rule) and row 208, which carries the rule and the six instances. Logs: `.superpowers/run-p-hard1-loop195.log`, `.superpowers/p-hard1-195-diag.txt`, `.superpowers/run-p-hard1-t3-clockprobe.log`. | S — reproduce under a loop, then either correct the assertion's premise or fix `updated_at`'s semantics | parity-only (test infrastructure; whether shipped behaviour is affected is exactly the open question) | 193 (same class, different file) |
| 196 | The region and continent packs group country NAMES; `origin_country` carries ISO codes, and no code→name table exists (CLOSED — the location-names phase) | Filed by the prefetch phase (Phase A) out of adjudication C8's re-file valve, because the two location packs `content`/`location` had been gated on row 189 and row 189 closed **without being able to help them**. Phase A shipped the `origin_country` enumeration and the `facts_family` builder that consumes it, so the machinery those packs were waiting on is done — and they still cannot ship, for a reason nobody had written down until the pack files were fetched whole. **The evidence, measured rather than recalled.** `defaults/movie/region.yml` and `defaults/movie/continent.yml` both declare `type: country` with `search_term: {default: country}` — they group **Plex's own `country` values, which are display names**. Their grouping tables carry **647 member strings between them and zero ISO codes** (23 `include` entries and 22 addon keys over 323 members for region; 6 and 5 over 324 for continent), and the tables deliberately carry alias SPELLINGS of the same country — `Türkiye` beside `Turkey`, `Soviet Union` beside `Russia`, five spellings of the US Minor Outlying Islands — precisely because they are matching free text a Plex agent emitted. A table of ISO codes would need none of that. TMDb's `origin_country`, the field this service now stores, is an array of **ISO-3166-1 alpha-2 codes**, confirmed by live capture and not assumed: the captured `/movie/{id}` and `/tv/{id}` payloads both read `origin_country: ["US"]` while the same payloads' `production_countries` spell the same country out as `{"iso_3166_1": "US", "name": "United States of America"}`. **The two do not join without a code→name table, and at filing nothing supplied one:** not the fetched pack files (no country data at all in `franchise.yml`; names only in the other two), and not this tree — the same absence row 190 recorded for languages. **So a flip would not have been a transcription.** It would have been upstream's grouping tables plus a mapping layer this service invented, which is exactly the silent substitution row 189 rules out when it is not declared, and exactly what `collections/catalog.py`'s provenance rule refuses. That is the gap the close below fills: the table is no longer invented, it is fetched bytes with provenance, so the flip IS a transcription plus a lookup through a stamped table. The packs therefore stayed GATED at the time of filing and re-pointed here rather than shipping an approximation under Kometa's name — the 10b pack discipline, "a pack that can't ship honestly re-files". **What closing this row took**, and it was a decision before it was any code: vendor (or fetch, e.g. TMDb's `/configuration/countries`) an ISO-3166-1 alpha-2 → name table, then decide which SIDE is normalised — map our codes up into upstream's display names so its 647 strings keep working, or rewrite both grouping tables into codes, which is a re-transcription of two files rather than a lookup. The `location_country` pack beside these two was unaffected and shipped throughout: it enumerates Plex's own `country` tag, so its keys are already the display names upstream's tables were written against. It is also why the two packs' descriptions have to say they are a DIFFERENT value set from that one — an operator can enable all three, and they disagree on exactly the co-productions they would notice. **CLOSED by the location-names phase, and the gate above is now answered rather than open.** The decision came first, as this row asked. The table is FETCHED — `/configuration/countries` (and `/configuration/languages` beside it), once, provenance-stamped, vendored by script as `collections/iso_names.py` — and the side normalised is OURS: `origin_country`'s codes map UP into TMDb's English names, so upstream's 647 member strings work verbatim and both grouping tables are transcribed byte-for-byte (`packs.py`, digest-guarded at two sites). The join was MEASURED per-name before either pack flipped (`docs/research/tmdb-iso-names/README.md`). The library-side join the plan's gate named turned out to have an empty denominator — production's `tmdb_origin_country` is `[]` on all 13590 rows because the drift sweep has not revisited anything since Phase A merged — so the controller re-scoped the gate to the FORWARD join, which answers the same semantic question from the half of the evidence that exists: **241 of 251** fetched TMDb country names (96.0%) land in `region.yml`'s member universe and the same **241/251** in `continent.yml`'s, 243/251 (96.8%) accent-folded, with the two miss lists identical name for name. All ten misses are spelling divergences for countries upstream does carry — `Faeroe Islands`/`Faroe Islands`, `Kyrgyz Republic`/`Kyrgyzstan`, and eight more — and **not one of the 251 is absent as a place**, so a ten-entry OURS-marked `COUNTRY_NAME_ALIASES` table bridges them, each pair derived by comparing the two fetched artifacts on disk rather than recalled. Anything still unmatched falls into `Other Regions`/`Other Continents` honestly — upstream's own leftovers rule. `location_region` and `location_continent` ship READY; the `location_country` pack beside them is untouched; all three descriptions disclose the shared titles, and the two new packs' descriptions state that the value sets disagree on co-productions | M — a vendored or fetched country-name table, one normalisation decision, then two pack transcriptions | parity-only (two packs that do not ship) | 189 (closed), 190 (the same absence for languages), 102 |
| 197 | The Plex-side batched metadata read, the credits cache, and where that cache lives (CLOSED — phase B) | Filed by the prefetch phase (Phase A) out of adjudication C1, which SPLIT something this table had been calling one thing. **What "the metadata-prefetch budget" turned out to be: two unrelated mechanisms wearing one name.** Rows 91, 96, 189 and 192 all carried the phrase — row 155 was named as its home by the others rather than being a carrier of it — and the recon that scoped Phase A found three of those consumers (189, 192, and the location packs) share ONE TMDb payload the facts pipeline was already fetching and discarding — a scalar or two per item, rideable for free. The remaining consumers are Plex-side, want a different shape of answer, and share nothing with that payload: row 155's six stranded attributes want per-item metadata Plex's section listing truncates or omits, row 91 wants `item.collections` per item, and row 194's people scan wants library-wide COUNTS. Phase A shipped the TMDb half and closed 189/192 on it. **This row is the other half, and it is a phase rather than a row's worth of work.** Three parts. **(1) The batched read.** `/library/metadata/{k1},{k2},{k3}` — the path-segment form; the query-parameter spelling that stood in row 155 until Phase A's wrap corrected it does not exist in vendored plexapi 4.18.2. Its cost is measured rather than guessed: **10 calls / 16.90s for all 1955 movies at chunk=200** (`.superpowers/sdd/archive/p9a-task-2-report.md` §2, phase 9a's own read-only probe — no probe in Phase A spoke to Plex at all). What that buys is the (a) half of row 155: `genre`, `label`, `collection`, `audio_language`, `subtitle_language`. It does not buy `network`, which Plex 1.43.4 emits nowhere and no budget can conjure — that half of 155 is a decision, not a fetch. **(2) The credits cache**, which is row 194's scan and, as a neighbour rather than a blocker, what row 169's four people attributes would read from. **(3) The decision this row exists to make BEFORE it is discovered: is that cache `item_facts`, or a table of its own?** Phase A deliberately did not prejudge it (adjudication C3 says so in as many words) and could not have: the three fields it added are scalars and fit a row on `item_facts` uncontroversially, while credits are many-to-many — one item has many people, one person is in many items — and a scalar table is the wrong shape for that whether or not it can be made to hold it. Deciding it late means either a migration that moves data or a second cache that quietly disagrees with the first, which is why it is written here as the first thing this phase does. **What this row does NOT inherit from Phase A:** the budget itself, because Plex is local and the cost being managed is reloads rather than a remote rate limit. What it CAN reuse in shape if it ever needs to is the pattern Phase A proved: a shared window on the database clock rather than a process variable, a refusal that never enters the cache, and an attempt stamped whether or not it found anything. **Not started, and deliberately its own recon-to-plan cycle** — Phase A's scope decision was that a phase which had already shipped a widening, a rate budget, an enumeration seam, a third family shape and a pack flip should stop rather than take on a second subsystem **CLOSED — phase B, which was that recon-to-plan cycle.** The batched read is `plex/client.fetch_tag_index` over `_iter_metadata_batches`, which hands plexapi's own list-of-ints `fetchItems` (it already translates one to `/library/metadata/{k1,k2,…}`, base.py:334-335 — the seam existed unused) in chunks of `TAG_BATCH_CHUNK = 200`, deduplicating keys first; the per-run enrichment cache is `collections/enrichment.ensure_tags`, memoised on the pass-level `run_cache` and scoped to the RESOLVED ITEM SET of the definitions that need it, never the whole library, with `test_call_count_is_ceil_n_over_chunk_exactly` pinning `ceil(1955/200) == 10` calls exactly. The chunk size needed no reduction: the phase-B probe measured 1200 keys / 7671 path characters working and ~1600 refused as a plexapi `BadRequest` carrying no status code, but found that COST binds long before the cap does — 8–12 ms/item at chunks of 50-200 against 15.5/25.2/36.7 at 400/800/1200 (`docs/research/plex-batch-probe/README.md`, probe c). **The cache-shape decision was taken as this row exists to take it, and it went to a DEDICATED table:** `item_credits`, composite PK `(item_id, kind, person)` with a CASCADE FK to `media_items` and an attempt stamp on the ITEM (`credits_attempted_at`), migration `a4db89c94eaa` — `item_facts` is scalar-per-item with a `UNIQUE(item_id)` and credits are many-to-many, so it was the wrong shape and answering that here rather than discovering it later is the whole reason this row was filed. Refusals are never cached; no row means ABSENT from every query, never a zero. The scan is `collections/credits.scan_library_credits` on a `credits_scan` scheduler job at `scheduler.credits_scan_days: 7`. **What the batched read may not claim:** Plex caps `<Role>` children at 200 per item server-side and a single-key fetch does not escape it (probe b: 54 of 200 sampled shows land on exactly 200), so anything counting credits reports a floor | L — its own phase: a recon, the cache-shape decision, the batched read with a reload budget, then the rows that unblock | parity-only (nothing ships today; six rows wait on it) | 155, 91, 194, 169 (neighbour), 96, 180 (destination for one branch of its walk) |
| 198 | `count_matches` fetches every matching item to answer "is this more than zero" (CLOSED — phase B) | Filed by the prefetch phase's wrap, and the reason it is a row is that **it has been deferred repeatedly and had nowhere to be deferred TO**. Originally 9c Task 2 Minor 7. What it is: `collections/smart.py:106-127`'s `count_matches` calls `section.fetchItems(…)` and returns `len(items)`, so validating a smart filter that matches four thousand items pulls four thousand items across the wire to learn that the answer is "more than none". Its only caller that needs the number as a *decision* is `require_matches` (`:130-148`), which refuses at zero and otherwise uses the count for one action string; the dynamic engine's `minimum_items` is the other caller and genuinely wants the number. **Two things keep it a Minor rather than a defect, and both were re-verified against shipped code when this row was filed rather than carried forward as claims.** It matches `plex_search.build`'s own precedent — `builders/plex_search.py:354` performs the identical unbounded `fetchItems` on the same URL shape — though the precedent is imperfect in this row's favour: `plex_search` NEEDS those items, because they are the collection's membership, and this call needs only their count. And it fires only on definition CHANGE: `smart.py:307` guards it with `if not definition_current`, so a pass over unchanged smart definitions pays nothing at all, and the cost lands on the pass after an operator edits a filter. **The deferral history, stated rather than implied.** What is on disk is one DEFER-with-destination in the 9c branch review — "follow-up row beside the metadata-prefetch/budget family (155/180); it matches `plex_search.build`'s own precedent and fires only on definition change" (`.superpowers/sdd/branch-review-9c.md:144`) — and one ledger line (`.superpowers/sdd/progress.md:2714`). No row in this table, through 9c, 10a-1, 10a-2, 10b, 10c-lite and Phase A's own planning; the phase-A adjudication that ordered it filed counts this as its **fifth** deferral. The destination it was given was "beside the metadata-prefetch/budget family", that family was Phase A, and this is Phase A's wrap — so it is filed here rather than deferred a sixth time, which is the whole point. **What closing it would take:** a count that does not materialise the items. A response with NO `totalSize` is a failure and not a zero, because reading one as the other would refuse a perfectly good filter at `require_matches`; zero MATCHES is an answer, an unreadable count is not. **The fifth deferral is DISCHARGED with it.** 9c's carry (`branch-review-9c.md:144`) was that `require_matches`' unbounded fetch had nowhere to be deferred to; it inherits the cheap read through this one function, as does the dynamic engine's `minimum_items` — no caller had to decide whether to share it, because the sharing was already the shape. `section._server` joins `_uriRoot` and the session in `tests/test_plexapi_collection_contract.py`, pinned along with `query`'s `headers=` parameter | S — one probe to confirm the container-size read, then one function; `minimum_items`' caller decides whether it shares it | parity-only (a cost, not a behaviour; the count it returns is correct today) | 197 (its filed neighbourhood), 102 |
| 199 | The franchise pack builds a family Kometa would not: no membership floor, and 29 collections short of upstream's members (CLOSED — the location-names phase, by decision) | Filed by the prefetch phase's wrap on row 190's precedent — "a divergence that lives only in a module docstring is one nobody finds" — and on its Task 4 review's ruling, which named two divergences of one class and said one row covers both. `content_franchises` ships READY on the `facts_family` / `tmdb_collection` enumeration row 192 closed, and it is a faithful transcription of `defaults/movie/franchise.yml`'s consumable tables: twelve `addons` pairs and three `title_override` entries, byte-for-byte and in file order, plus upstream's own `remove_suffix: Collection` and `custom_keys: false`. **Three of upstream's keys are not ported, and two of the three change what an operator's collections CONTAIN.** **(1) `minimum_items: 2`.** Upstream builds no collection for a franchise the library holds one film of; this service builds one. The knob is not merely unimplemented — `FactsFamilyParams`' docstring refuses it and prices the refusal: a unit's membership is resolved by the unit's OWN builder one layer down, so a per-key minimum here would mean resolving every key's membership twice, and for `tmdb_collection` that second pass is a TMDb read per franchise taken *before* deciding whether the franchise is worth building. A network cost, not a filter. The divergence is operator-visible on any library with many singletons, and it **interacts with the pack's `max_collections: 250`** — singleton franchises consume that budget, and the budget's failure mode is a loud refusal that creates nothing, so enough singletons can stop the family building at all. **That refusal is not free.** The franchise family names every enumerated key *before* the cap is checked (`builders/facts_family.py:288-311`, then `:352`) — one `/collection/{id}` read each, provider-cached per TTL — so a library over the cap re-pays the whole naming cost on every pass the cache has expired, refusing loudly and correctly each time. An early bound is awkward rather than impossible: `addons:` can merge keys down below the cap, so the only sound early refusal is on `len(counted) - merged_addon_keys > max_collections`, which is the fix if anyone ever wants it. That cap is itself the pack's one unevidenced number, disclosed as a judgement at its site and anchored at five times the builder's default of 50, because the value set is this service's own `item_facts` and no probe has ever counted franchises on a real library. **(2) `template_variables.movie` — 29 keys, 44 members.** Upstream merges loose movie ids into 29 of its franchise collections (Hobbs & Shaw into The Fast and the Furious, Once Upon a Deadpool into X-Men); this service does not, so those 29 collections are missing films Kometa puts in them. Not portable at the params layer: membership here is the franchise's own `parts` through a builder that takes one collection id, so there is no seam a loose id could be added at. **(3) `name_mapping`** is upstream's asset-FOLDER name and has no meaning in a service that resolves posters through its own providers — listed for completeness; it changes nothing an operator sees. **A fourth divergence is structural rather than an omission**, and is already stated in the preset's own description: an `addons` bucket merging two franchises builds the FIRST and names the rest in the pass report, because the shipped `tmdb_collection` builder takes a single id. Twelve buckets can reach that shape, and only on a library holding both halves. **One further edge belongs beside these rather than under them**, because it is a failure mode rather than a divergence: a TMDb 404 on `/collection/{id}` leaves a franchise unnameable, so its key derives no title and is absent from an otherwise-successful family's `generated` set — the one PER-KEY erosion path the family-level sweep protection does not cover, since that protection is all-or-nothing per family. It is bounded (`delete_unconfigured` is off by default, `max_deletes` caps any sweep, and the pass names the `title_override` that titles the key by hand), but `providers/fetch.py:44-45` negative-caches a 404 for the TTL, so a transient upstream 404 keeps the key dropped for about a day rather than for one pass. **Why this is filed rather than fixed.** Every one of these is disclosed at its transcription site and in the operator-facing preset description — which is already better than the module docstring row 190 was filed about — and a test asserts the description still states each pinned opinion, so the prose cannot drift from the params. What no code path carries is the count: nothing tells an operator that 29 of their franchise collections are smaller than the same pack under Kometa. **What closing it takes** is a decision before it is code: whether parity here is worth a `minimum_items` that costs a TMDb read per franchise per pass, and whether the 44 loose ids are worth a second membership source in a family whose whole design is "the franchise's own parts". Both are legitimately answerable "no" — in which case this row closes by being read, not by shipping. **CLOSED by being read, at the location-names wrap, exactly as the clause above allowed — and read means re-verified, not recalled.** All three unported keys' disclosures were checked standing in the shipped tree before the decision was taken: the `FRANCHISE_PARAMS` trailing comment block still names all three with their reasons (`collections/packs.py:1480-1502`), `FactsFamilyParams`' docstring still prices the `minimum_items` refusal in the terms this row quotes — the count lives one layer down, so a per-key minimum resolves every key's membership twice (`builders/facts_family.py:136-142`) — and the operator-facing `content_franchises` description still states the missing floor, the twelve addon buckets that build their first half, and the 29 collections short (`collections/catalog.py:1134-1149`). Nothing had drifted, so the row had a live decision to make rather than a re-point to file. **Both decisions land "no."** A `minimum_items` that costs a TMDb read per franchise per pass buys a filter by paying a network price on every item it will then discard, which is worse than the divergence it removes — and the divergence is disclosed in the place an operator actually reads. A second membership source for 44 loose ids, in a family whose whole design is "the franchise's own parts through one collection id", buys 29 slightly fuller collections by putting two definitions of membership into one builder — also worse than the disclosed divergence. `name_mapping` never had a question to answer: there is no asset-folder concept here to map to. The row closes with the divergences intact, named, and pinned by the test that asserts the description still states each one | S — a decision on each, then either a `FactsFamilyParams` field with its cost accepted or a documented refusal | parity-only (membership differs from upstream; nothing is wrong, and both differences are disclosed) | 192 (closed), 190 (the precedent for filing a visible divergence) |

Row 49 shipped `collections.group_order` as YAML only; the Settings page rendered it read-only (a null default falls through the generic editor's widget chooser), and nothing in the frontend knew the groups existed. **What shipped.** `GET /api/collections/catalog` gains a `groups` array — `{key, title, section, position}` per group, derived from `collections/groups.py`'s one pure new function (`group_listing`) and served in the RUNNING config's effective order, so the ten keys are never transcribed into TypeScript (the keys live in exactly one place, and a frontend holding its own copy of the ten names would be the drift this module's other tables refuse; a four-group test fixture proves the panel assumes nothing about the count either). | 204 | `filters:` normalises no values while `plex_search` resolves them, so the same attribute name takes two value vocabularies | Filed by phase B's wrap out of the Task 3 review's controller adjudication, as row 190's precedent demands: a divergence that lives only in a module docstring is one nobody finds. **The mechanism.** The batched accessor hands back whatever the enrichment carries, and for the language rows that is Plex's `languageTag` — ISO 639-1, `en`.
