The identical spelling in a `plex_search` block works. **Introduced by phase B in the sense that matters:** before it, `audio_language` in a `filters:` block refused outright, so there was no second vocabulary to disagree with. The asymmetry is a consequence of two individually-correct choices — the batched read stores codes, and the accessor performs no request — and the second is the load-bearing one: resolving `English` → `en` the way `plex_search` does means asking the server for its choice list, which is the ONE thing the tier-1 accessor layer may never do (`filter_values.py:8`). **Current cover:** the two language rows in `collections/filters.py` state that the values are ISO 639-1 codes and tell the operator to write `audio_language: en`. **What closing it would take:** a value-normalisation seam on the `filters:` side that is offline by construction — a `langcodes`-backed base-code fold would answer `eng` and `pt-BR` without a request; `English` needs a name table this tree does not have, which is the same table row 190's language TITLES lack and row 196's country NAMES lack, so the three are neighbours — that table exists now (`collections/iso_names.py`), so the residue is one seam, not a table. **The code-spelling half SHIPPED with the location-names phase:** `filters:` now compares the two language attributes at the BASE code — `base_language_code` moved from `builders/plex_search.py` into `collections/filters.py`, because the model layer must not import a builder, and both sides of the tag comparison fold through it — so `audio_language: eng` answers, and a written base code meets a regional stream tag (`pt` against `pt-BR`), offline by construction, zero Plex reads. One judgement rides with it, disclosed at the site and pinned by test: a regional WRITTEN value (`es-419`) matches at its base here, where `plex_search` targets an exact library value only. That is a deliberate widening on this side, not an oversight, and it is the one place the two vocabularies still differ in the other direction. **The display-title half stays OPEN in this row** — `audio_language: English` still matches nothing — but what it waits on has changed: no longer a table this tree lacks, only a seam that consults the one it now has, `collections/iso_names.py`'s `LANGUAGE_NAMES`, vendored by row 196's fetch | S — the offline code fold SHIPPED; the display-title residue is one seam over an existing table | parity-only (the block loads and answers; only the spelling an operator guesses decides whether it answers right) | 155 (closed — the widening), 190 (closed), 196 (closed — the fetch that supplied the table), 96 |
| 205 | `ElementTree.ParseError` escapes the narrowed exception classes at both stream-read sites (CLOSED — hardening sweep 1) | Filed by the location-names wrap from branch-review-phaseb D1, which was ruled and never filed; the T2 review's item 9 raised the same escape independently, so this is one finding sighted twice rather than a new one. **The mechanism, read out of the installed dependency rather than assumed.** plexapi's `utils.parseXMLString` (`plexapi/utils.py:836-844`, v4.18.2) catches `ParseError` only to RETRY `ElementTree.fromstring` on a cleaned string, and that retry is UNGUARDED — a body that is not XML at all fails the clean-and-retry the same way it failed the first parse, and the second `ParseError` propagates. `ElementTree.ParseError` is a `SyntaxError` subclass (`ParseError → SyntaxError → Exception`), so it is in NEITHER caught class at `collections/enrichment.py:73` nor at `builders/plex_search.py:472` — both catch `(PlexApiException, requests.RequestException)`. The realistic trigger is a reverse proxy or captive portal answering **200 with an HTML error body**: the transport succeeded, so `requests` raises nothing, and plexapi never gets far enough to raise a `PlexApiException`. **The comment at the second site is actively misleading and should be corrected in the same edit:** `plex_search.py:462-464` says "a malformed response or an auth failure is `PlexApiException` itself", which is true for the auth half and false for the malformed half — malformed is exactly the case that escapes. **Why this is hardening and not a correctness bug.** Every shipped consumer contains the escape in a blanket engine/job catch, so the failure becomes a refusal either way and no missing-data-becomes-wrong-write path exists; what is lost is the NON-MEMOISATION (the pass re-attempts a read that will fail again, where a caught failure is cached for the pass) and the log copy — the operator gets a raw traceback instead of the named refusal both sites were written to produce. **The fix adds `ElementTree.ParseError` to the tuple at BOTH sites TOGETHER**, with a red test per site. The class lists are deliberately coupled — enrichment's catch was written from plex_search's and its comment cites that file by line — so correcting one alone breaks the coupling and leaves the next reader with two near-identical tuples that disagree for no recorded reason. Nothing was fixed by the filing phase. **closed hardening-sweep-1:** `ElementTree.ParseError` added to the tuple at BOTH sites in one commit — `collections/enrichment.py:82` and `builders/plex_search.py:479`, identical and in the same order — with the stale malformed-response clause corrected in the same edit (and two further stale phrasings downstream of it, which the brief had not named), plus a red test per site: each showed a raw `xml.etree.ElementTree.ParseError` propagating where the named refusal belonged. **On the report's absolute that "no test can catch drift":** half of it was wrong and that half is now pinned. `0561412` `ast`-parses both modules, identifies each site by a semantic property (the tuple-typed handler that names `PlexApiException` itself, which excludes `plex_search`'s `NotFound`/`BadRequest` handler without depending on ordering) and asserts the two class-name sets are equal — so **drift between the two known sites is pinned**, and its mutation names the offending file. The other half stands: a THIRD stream-read site added later is a review-time property that neither the ast test nor a shared constant can see, and the mechanisms that could are the over-engineering C2 warns off. A shared `_STREAM_READ_FAILURES` constant was considered and rejected on those C2 grounds — it would buy an import edge from a builder into `enrichment.py` for something the ast test gets free. | S — one exception class at two named sites, one stale comment clause, plus a red test per site | hardening (a refusal arrives as a raw traceback rather than a named refusal, and is re-attempted rather than memoised) | phase-B branch review §2/§7 (D1, ruled and never filed), location-names T2 review item 9 |
| 206 | The Phase-A facts columns converge over weeks; nothing offers a one-time catch-up sweep (CLOSED — sweep 2) | Filed by the location-names wrap as the controller's declared default, because the phase shipped two packs whose membership depends on a column that is empty today and fills slowly. **The measurement, not an estimate.** Production's `tmdb_origin_country` is `[]` on all 13590 `item_facts` rows and `tmdb_original_language` is NULL on all of them, re-checked twice against the live database (`docs/research/tmdb-iso-names/README.md` §2, captures at 2026-08-30 06:59Z and 08:56Z, byte-identical). The cause is measured and benign: the ratings-drift sweep is the only thing that revisits a fact row, every row was written in the 08-23…08-28 backfill window, and the sweep only picks up rows older than `drift_max_age_days` — so at the re-check **0 of 2252** candidates were eligible, with the first eligible at `2026-08-30 21:50Z`. The writer is provably healthy (12458 of 13590 rows carry a `tmdb`-sourced field); the columns are never-written, not written-empty. **The pace.** The sweep's population is `kind IN ('movie','show')` = **2252 items**, counted rather than inferred, and it enqueues at most `scheduler.drift_batch_size` = 500 per `scheduler.drift_days` = 7 tick, so a full revisit is `ceil(2252/500)` = **5 ticks ≈ 5 weeks** at the defaults. An operator who enables `location_region`, `location_continent` or the `original_language` family on a settled library therefore watches real-but-small collections grow for about a month, which the three pack descriptions disclose. **The workaround that exists today**, and it is why this is a convenience row rather than a defect: the two knobs above are live config — raising `drift_batch_size` or lowering `drift_days` compresses the same five ticks into days, and lowering `drift_max_age_days` makes rows eligible sooner. Nothing is broken; the default cadence is simply tuned for ratings drift, which is not urgent, and is now also carrying a one-time schema fill, which is. **What closing it takes:** a one-shot catch-up job that walks the parent population once, ignoring the age threshold, rate-limited against TMDb and resumable, triggered on demand rather than scheduled — the drift sweep's own `sweep_stale_facts` with the age predicate dropped and a cursor, not a new pipeline. The open design question is what triggers it: a migration hook fires it for everyone including operators who do not want it, so a UI button or a CLI entry point is the likelier shape. **closed sweep-2:** the row's own design shipped — `backfill_facts` (`scheduler/jobs.py`) is `sweep_stale_facts`' population, valve, stamp and enqueue with the age predicate dropped and a `facts_backfill_state` cursor (walking `MediaItem.id`) in its place; the trigger adjudication landed as the ops-precedent `POST /api/facts/backfill` + a `GET` for standing progress + one button on the Collections page (`FactsBackfillPanel`), NO migration hook per this row's own rejection. Each press enqueues one `drift_batch_size` batch through the normal pipeline; a re-press resumes from the cursor; a drained population answers complete idempotently; an open `TmdbRateBudget` window PARKS the trigger, cursor unmoved, because a batch gathered with TMDb skipped would stamp `fetched_at` and silently lose the columns this exists to fill. Progress is always the live parent count, never this row's measured 2252 | S–M — one job reusing the sweep's query without its age predicate, a cursor, and a trigger | convenience (the facts converge either way; this decides whether it takes days or weeks) | 196 (the packs that wait on the fill), 190, 192 |
| 207 | Credential tracebacks still reach stdout, and `/api/logs` still shows the operator's base URL (CLOSED — sweep 3) | Filed by hardening sweep 1's wrap out of Task 1's review (Minor 1), because row 117 closed on the SERVED surface only and the remainder deserves a row rather than a memory. **What row 117 fixed:** `LogBuffer.emit` scrubs `...api_key`/`apikey`/`token=` query params out of every formatted record before it reaches `/api/logs` or the SSE stream. **What it deliberately did not, and why that is a row.** (a) **stdout.** `main.py:74`'s `logging.basicConfig` installs a root handler that formats independently of the `LogBuffer` handler `app.py:158` adds, so `providers/ladder.py:81` and `candidates.py`'s two fan-out sites still print the full `exc_info` traceback — Fanart's `api_key` included — to the pod log. The repo already holds itself to a stricter rule than that: `main.py:75-81` mutes httpx to WARNING with the words "Neither may reach the pod logs", and the sweep's own constraint reads "tokens/keys never printed". (b) **the operator's base URL.** Row 117's pattern redacts the credential PARAM and leaves the host, so `/api/logs` still serves `http://plex.internal:32400/library/sections/9/all` — which the same constraint's "operator URLs never in logs" clause touches. **Why it is not a one-line fix.** The unscrubbed stdout copy is not an oversight; it is the compensating control rows 136/188 lean on, pinned by `tests/test_scheduler_collections_job.py:285-286` (the token and the cause are both asserted present in `caplog.text`). Any fix must not blind that, so the real question is whether per-sink formatters (scrub-everything for the buffer, full detail for stdout) are the answer, or whether the pod log is accepted as a trusted sink and the rule reworded. Two smaller notes from the same review belong here: the pattern matches `api_key`/`apikey` but not `api-key` (no shipped provider spells it that way, so not live — `api[-_]?key` would close it), and `_scrub` now runs on every record through the process-global root handler, so a pathological long word-character run is rescanned from every offset. **A third note, from the hardening-sweep-1 polish pass:** a percent-encoded `api_key%3D…` — a URL nested inside another URL's query value, so the literal `=` the pattern requires is itself encoded — would pass the scrub unredacted; no shipped provider produces that shape today, so it is not live, but it belongs beside the `api-key` note above if this row is ever worked. Nothing was changed by the filing. **closed sweep-3, on the sink decision:** the pod log is the TRUSTED sink — full detail belongs to stdout (host-only, kubectl-gated), and every served surface carries redacted text; per-sink formatters are NOT built (the one-seam `LogBuffer` scrub is the precedent; a formatter matrix is the YAGNI the standing laws forbid). Under that decision this row's **(a) half is ACCEPTED, plainly: the `exc_info` tracebacks that reach the pod log CARRYING KEYS stay whole** — that unscrubbed stdout copy is the compensating control rows 136/188 lean on, caplog-pinned, and the sweep constraint's "never printed"/"never in logs" wording is hereby read as governing the SERVED surfaces. What closed is the (b) half plus the pattern notes: `_scrub` now also redacts the authority of every URL-shaped host at the same `LogBuffer` seam (`http://REDACTED/library/...`, the path deliberately kept — it is what makes a line debuggable, and the clause is about hosts; public API hosts over-match on purpose, row 117's tradeoff, so `/api/logs` alone no longer tells an operator WHICH provider a bare `https://REDACTED/3/movie/603` came from), `api[-_]?key` closes the api-key spelling note, and `(=\|%3D)` closes the percent-encoded evasion note. The rescan-performance note stays accepted as filed — the added alternations do not change the every-record posture. **A SECOND residual is accepted with the first, and named as plainly: the percent-encoding defence is one level deep, and it covers the CREDENTIAL half only.** Two executed shapes: a double-encoded `?src=…%253Fapi_key%253DSECRETKEY` serves the secret intact (`%25` matches neither `=` nor `%3D`), and — the asymmetry that matters — a nested `?src=http%3A%2F%2Fplex.internal%3A32400%2Fx` carries the OPERATOR HOST through unredacted, because the host clause is anchored on a literal `://` that an encoded URL does not contain. So the two halves of this row now differ on exactly the shape the new `%3D` test dramatises: the nested credential is caught, the nested host is not. Filed as **row 212** rather than fixed here — closing it means decoding before scrubbing or a second encoded-scheme alternation, a design choice that deserves its own adjudication, and C3 forbade the scope. **A THIRD residual, live rather than probed: the same host pattern's literal `://` anchor also misses the scheme-less operator-host shape `requests`' own connection-error messages produce, so a Plex-outage traceback still serves the host on `/api/logs` today — filed as row 214.** One further note, cosmetic rather than a residual leak: the host class does not exclude trailing punctuation, so a path-less authority at the end of a sentence eats the period (`failed at http://plex.internal:32400.` → `failed at http://REDACTED`). It always over-consumes, never under-redacts, so it cannot leak; the fix, if ever wanted, is a trailing-punctuation trim weighed against widening the pattern for no security gain | S — a sink decision first, then a small edit either way | none (a residual leak, not a feature) | 117; 136/188 lean on the residual |
| 208 | Suite discipline: no test may order, or narrowly bound, two wall-clock readings | Filed by hardening sweep 1's wrap as the durable half of row 195's investigation. The other half — "the developer's VM has a broken clock" — is not durable work product: it is not a repo change, CI cannot verify it, it has no closing condition, and it rots the moment the machine is reimaged. **The enforceable rule, which is this row:** no test in this suite may assert an ORDERING, or a tolerance under about three seconds, between two wall-clock readings taken at different instants — database clock or host clock alike. It is checkable at review time, it survives a machine change, and it has a measured justification. **The evidence.** `docs/research/dev-clock-step/README.md` carries the measurement (a 120 s probe: 11 646 samples, 4 step events, Postgres `clock_timestamp()` and the container's `time.time()` retreating by the same amount at the same sample, agreeing to under 100 µs), the derivation (a composite **-2.705 s** step every **~30 s** of REAL time; the 27.28 s figure the probe prints is apparent time, short by exactly one step per cycle), the reproducer inline so it cannot evaporate with the gitignored path it was run from, and the immune shapes — `time.perf_counter()`, and any comparison made within a single transaction, where `transaction_timestamp()` is fixed at transaction start and cannot move underneath the reader. Exposure is `interval / 30 s`, where `interval` is the gap between the two readings; the step magnitude only supplies the CONDITION (it inverts any ordering whose interval is under 2.7 s). **The instances, all found and all handled during the sweep:** six assertions across four files — `tests/test_pipeline.py`'s row-195 assertion (~1.12 s interval, ~3.7 %), `tests/test_facts_gather.py:301` and `:340` (~0.1 s, ~0.3 % each), and `tests/test_item_facts.py:69` plus `tests/test_queue.py:119`, whose 1 s tolerances were smaller than the step. The first three were relaxed to re-stamp equality; the last two were RESTRUCTURED onto same-transaction identity rather than widened, which made them strictly stronger — their mutations land 3.5 ms and 374 µs off, so the tolerance they replaced would have passed both defects. **Five of the six are now immune. The sixth is an irreducible residual, and that is the interesting part of this row.** `tests/test_queue.py:309` compares a `run_after` stamped inside `reclaim_stale()`'s transaction against a later `now()`, and `reclaim_stale()` COMMITS — so the comparison is inherently cross-transaction and no test-level restructure removes it. Computing it server-side moves where the comparison happens, not which two readings it compares. It matters beyond the test: `_CLAIM_SQL` matches on `run_after <= now()` and `_RECLAIM_SQL` on `claimed_at < now() - make_interval(...)`, both against rows stamped in earlier committed transactions, so a backwards step in that window mis-schedules real work — a job not yet due, a claim not yet stale. At an order-of-magnitude estimate of ~2 ms — the one measurement on hand is 5.6 ms (`run_after 11:59:33.478596` vs `db_now 11:59:33.484209`, `run-p-hard1-t4-pre-mut.log:50`) — against a ~30 s cycle that is ≲0.02 % (5.6 ms / 30 s ≈ 0.019 %, not the ~0.007 % the 2 ms estimate implied) and has never been sighted, but it is the APPLICATION's exposure, not the suite's. **So the rule bans avoidable cross-transaction wall-clock assertions; it cannot ban the ones that are the behaviour under test**, and a close that claimed "six of six immune" would have been a wrong record. Two sites were checked and deliberately left alone, recorded so the next sweep does not re-derive it: `tests/test_queue.py:92` and `tests/test_routes.py:131` both compare `run_after` against a LATER `db_now` with seconds of slack, and a backwards-only step makes `db_now` smaller, which only makes them easier to satisfy. **Scope caveat, carried verbatim from the investigation:** this is a developer-environment fact, not a known CI fact — a Linux runner may well be monotonic. The suite is written to survive both, which is why the rule is about the assertions and not about anyone's machine. An operator on this machine can look at Docker Desktop / WSL2 time sync (`wsl --shutdown`), and nothing here depends on their doing so. **No outstanding code:** the six sites are handled and the residual is named; what remains is the rule, applied at review time | S — the code is done; this row is the rule plus its evidence | parity-only (test infrastructure) | 195 |

Three other served-column writers still carry the raw exception string the class-name-only rule now argues against. **(1) `scheduler/core.py:169`.** Any non-collections scheduled job's `except Exception as error` branch sets `status, detail = "failed", str(error)`, and `:181` writes `row.last_detail = detail[:2000]`, served by `/api/snapshots`. **(2) `queue/worker.py:116` and `:128`.** Both `fail(session, job_id, str(exc), ...)` calls write the bare exception string to `job.last_error`, served at `api/jobs.py:146` (`"last_error": None if waiting else job.last_error`). **(3) `api/mismatches.py:209`.** `raise HTTPException(status_code=502, detail=f"{service} did not answer: {exc}")` carries `exc`'s own text — an `httpx.HTTPError`, whose string form embeds the request URL — so a Radarr/Sonarr outage's 502 detail can carry the operator's Arr base URL. **Why this is filed rather than fixed.** All three are credential-safe today: Arr authentication rides the `X-Api-Key` header (verified against `ArrClient`), never the URL, so mismatches.py's exposure is a base URL, not a secret, and the scheduler/worker sites carry whatever the underlying exception's `str()` happens to hold, which for this codebase's failure modes has not been observed to include a credential. But all three violate the class-name-only rule the sweep established at its one boundary, and rows 136/188's own argument for narrowing does not stop at that boundary's edge. **What closing it would take:** the same class-name-only treatment at each of the three sites, plus deciding whether `mismatches.py`'s 502 detail should drop the URL entirely or keep it (an operator debugging "Radarr did not answer" plausibly wants to know which URL failed, unlike the collections job's internal error). **closed sweep-3:** class-name-only at all three sites, the rows-136/188 rule extended to the boundary's siblings. **(1) `scheduler/core.py`'s failure branch** serves `type(error).__name__`, EXCEPT an exception marking itself `served_detail = True` — `CollectionsPassFailed` and `PruneRefused`, whose messages are BUILT for the served surfaces and are redaction-reviewed at their construction sites. The full text stays on the same site's `exc_info` warning, now caplog-pinned; the notification payload narrows with it (same string). **(2) `queue/worker.py`:** the Plex-outage branch serves the class name alone (requests' `str()` embeds the host and port); the deferral branch serves `ClassName: message`, the message kept because both `ItemNotFound` raise sites interpolate only item-identifying fields — the job's own title and ids at `plex/client.py:635`, a Plex-side `rating_key` at `:645` — with no host, URL, token or file path in either, which is the `PlexPathMismatch` branch's shape. **The compensating control at both worker sites is the shipped INFO lines carrying the full message, caplog-pinned — it is NOT `logger.exception`:** the filing's wording said the full detail would be logged via `logger.exception`, and the adjudication reads that as the full message via the shipped INFO controls. `main.py`'s `basicConfig` ships INFO, so the pod log genuinely gets the whole string; what it does not get at these two sites is a traceback, which a deferral and an outage do not need. **(3) `api/mismatches.py`'s 502 detail** is now `"<service> did not answer (<ClassName>)"` — the disclosure decision landed as DROP: the `api/pick` 502 precedent, with which-URL answered by the warning's traceback in the pod log. | 211 | Divider polish: `facts_family` units misroute to `!100_` (65 live franchises, unheaded); seven groups' dividers artless; a style change could never re-poster; no franchises group; no fence against Plex's own tail (CLOSED — the divider-polish phase) | Filed and closed by the same phase (`feat/divider-polish`), on four items the user read off the live collections tab plus a fifth added mid-thread. **The bug behind item 1, which the recon found and nobody had filed.** `facts_family` units fall through `group_for` to the OPERATOR group because both their title AND their builder differ from the placeholder definition that expanded them — so 65 live franchise collections sat in `!100_`, under the operator block, with no heading above them. Fixed by threading the placeholder's group through expansion (`collections/engine.py`) rather than adding per-builder rows to `_BUILTIN_GROUPS`, which would have been a second registry to drift. **Item 2 — art for every divider — shipped as the HYBRID.** Three groups (`charts`, `awards`, `content_ratings`) keep upstream's shipped separator JPEGs because upstream's baked-in word matches our divider's title exactly; every other divider (the seven artless groups, `franchises`, and the fence) is GENERATED — our own title composited onto upstream's textless `@base/<style>.png` layer by `collections/separator_art.py`, cached under `<assets_root>/.generated/separators/`. Mapping to near-miss art was rejected outright: upstream names its separators by defaults FILE, not by category, so the closest match would have put GENRE on a divider titled "Content Collections". The generation parameters are TRANSCRIBED, not guessed or measured — `Kometa-Team/Defaults-Image-Creation@a9e02e9` (MIT © 2025 Kometa Team) `create_default_posters.ps1:5531`: Comfortaa-Medium, `#FFFFFF`, ALL CAPS, a `1900x1000` caption box trimmed and re-extented, gravity-centre onto the `2000x3000` base, point size clamped `[100,203]` and therefore always 203 because `caption:` word-wraps rather than shrinking. The transcription was VERIFIED by re-rendering upstream's own shipped `genre.jpg` from its own `@base/orig.png`: RMSE **0.000342** normalised, against controls at 0.0858 (±8pt) and 0.1239 (+47pt) — a reproduction, not a resemblance. `Comfortaa-Medium.ttf` is vendored under `assets/fonts/` with `PROVENANCE.md` recording the URL and sha256; the face is **upstream's, transcribed**, not ours (`.superpowers/sdd/p-div-font.md` §7). A divider whose art can neither be fetched nor rendered reports `no poster source` and retries next pass — never a broken write. **Item 3 — the style select — shipped as `collections.separator_style`**, one of upstream's 22 `sep_style` names, default `orig` (upstream's own default and the shipped value), governing BOTH art kinds, with a 22-swatch grid preview in the Groups panel. The trap the recon found and this closes: the separator's poster key carried no style, so before this a style change would have re-written the config and **never re-postered anything**. The style is now folded key-into-hash, so a change rewrites once and settles. **Item 4 — the fence — shipped as the pinned `!999_` "Other Collections" divider**, a pseudo-group `other` deliberately OUTSIDE `CANONICAL_ORDER`: it has no members, takes no position, `group_order` cannot name it, and a twelfth group renumbers everything except it. It is sweep-protected by construction, its title refuses by name at config load (`config/schema.py:910` — the likeliest collision deserves the helpful refusal), and it heads Plex's own auto-generated tail. **Item 5 was NO ACTION and that is the finding:** People containing both actors and directors is rightful, not a defect. **The churn, COUNTED from the live census rather than estimated:** 144 `editSortTitle` PUTs + 2 creates — 79 from the renumber (media→`!070` 36, people→`!080` 43; location/production/time hold no live rows) and 65 franchise members moving out of `!100_` into the new `!050` block — plus the C4 key-format change's one-time re-hash of every live divider. All of it is ONE pass, then settled, and it is disclosed as one story in `deploy/README.md`; the "zero churn on upgrade" framing an earlier draft used was retired as false-as-worded (the style DEFAULT adds nothing beyond the fold's single accounted rewrite, but the fold itself is a rewrite). Row 49's `!110_→!030_` move is the exact precedent. **Licensing is a three-way split and is never conflated:** the generation PARAMETERS are MIT (`Defaults-Image-Creation`); the IMAGES (`Default-Images`, both the shipped JPEGs and the `@base` layers) carry NO licence deliberately — per the maintainer on Discord, 2026-08-29, *"nearly all the default images are based on other work, so I'm not sure it's reasonable or valid to apply a license to derivative works"*; and Comfortaa is OFL by its own author, independently of both, which is what makes vendoring the `.ttf` clean. Generating rather than fetching buys an exact label, NOT a cleaner licence posture — only the caption layer is ours. **Deliberately untouched:** universes stay in `content` (Kometa files them there and they are not 65 rows); row 199's franchise `minimum_items` is a separate gap; and rows 49 and 104 are CLOSED predecessors this row succeeds — the scheme it renumbers and the prefix path it writes through — not reopened. | 212 | The `/api/logs` scrub's percent-encoding defence is one level deep, and covers the credential half only (CLOSED — sweep 5) | Filed by sweep 3's wrap out of Task 1's review (Minor 1), because row 207 closed having ACCEPTED this residual and an accepted-but-unfiled gap is one nobody finds. **The mechanism.** Both patterns in `api/logs.py` are anchored on literal characters an encoded URL does not contain. `_CREDENTIAL_PARAM` matches `=` or `%3D` — one level of encoding — so a double-encoded `%253D` passes it; `_URL_HOST` is anchored on a literal `://`, so a URL nested inside another URL's query value (`?src=http%3A%2F%2Fplex.internal%3A32400%2Fx`) keeps its authority on the served surface. **The asymmetry is why this is a row rather than a note:** the nested-URL shape is exactly what row 207's new `%3D` test dramatises, and after that fix the nested CREDENTIAL is redacted while the nested HOST beside it is not. **Not live today** — no shipped provider or client produces a doubly-nested URL in a logged message; the shapes above were found by adversarial probing of the compiled patterns, not in a captured log line, which is why they were accepted rather than fixed mid-sweep. **What closing it would take, and why it wants an adjudication rather than an executor's judgement:** either unquote the text before scrubbing (catches every depth in one move, but changes what the served line LOOKS like — an operator would read a decoded URL where the logging site wrote an encoded one, and the scrub would stop being a pure redaction) or add an encoded-scheme alternation to the host pattern (`https?(://\|%3A%2F%2F)` — leaves the text alone, but buys exactly one more level, and the same question returns at `%25`). The pod log keeps the full line under row 207's trusted-sink decision either way, so nothing here is urgent. **closed sweep-5:** by the alternation, not the unquote — decode-before-scrub changes what the served line looks like and rewrites literal %XX in arbitrary log text, breaking the doctrine's "same text, less of it" shape, so the adjudication took the same move row 207 made for the credential half, one level up: the host scrub gains a second, encoded-scheme pattern beside the untouched `_URL_HOST` (`_URL_HOST_ENCODED`, matching `https?%3A%2F%2F`), and the encoded-authority match terminates at `%2F` so the nested URL's encoded path survives exactly as the plain path does (the path-survives pin extends to the encoded form). Seen RED on the served surface — `plex.internal` served whole inside the nested shape — before landing green (`run-p-sweep5-t1-red.log`). The tempering is scoped to the ENCODED branch as a SEPARATE pattern (`_URL_HOST_ENCODED`), because sharing one character class between both branches — as the fix first shipped — also stopped the PLAIN branch at any literal `%2F` in the authority (percent-encoded userinfo), serving a host that `c79d7f4` redacted whole; that regression was caught in review and fixed by splitting the patterns, with a 200 000-case differential fuzz confirming the plain form is exactly as untempered as it was at `c79d7f4`. **The residuals, accepted and named on the row's own terms.** (1) **Double-encoding.** `%253A` passes — the alternation buys exactly one level and the same question returns at the next — no shipped client produces it, and the pod log keeps the full line under the trusted-sink decision. (2) **The quoted-host fail-open**, new with the row-214 half and documented in the pattern's own comment: `_HOST_KEYWORD`'s value class is tempered against comma, space and the closing quote, so a quoted value CONTAINING a comma or space, or an unterminated quote, makes the whole `host=` match fail and the value is served INTACT — a fail-open, not a truncation. Adjudicated acceptable because `requests` never writes a hostname shaped that way, so no live carrier is affected. (3) **The quote-safe semi-encoded shape.** `urllib.parse.quote`'s default `safe='/'` leaves the slashes literal and encodes only the colon, so quoting a URL yields `http%3A//host%3A32400/x` — matched by NEITHER `_URL_HOST` (needs a literal `://`) NOR `_URL_HOST_ENCODED` (needs `%3A%2F%2F`). Probed, not sighted: no call to `quote(` exists anywhere in `src/`, and the pod log keeps the full line either way | S — one pattern alternation or one unquote seam, plus the choice between them | none (a residual leak on the served surface, not a feature) | 207 (closed — the row whose close accepted this residual), 117 |
